O_{2}
respectively. If this network is embedded in spacetime, our relativistic causality condition (
Definition

23
) would require at least one of
A
or
C
to be embedded in the future light-cone of
B
(since
\{B\}
signals to
\{A,C\}
), the spacetime embedding of (a) for this network therefore violates the relativistic causality condition of the present paper, although it respects the strictly weaker relativistic causality condition of
VilasiniColbeckPRA
;
VilasiniColbeckPRL
and does not lead to superluminal signalling as discussed in the text (and shown in
VilasiniColbeckPRL
.

The difference between the two compatibility conditions, which correspond to two distinct (but related) relativistic causality principles as outlined above, leads to different characterisations of the set of information processing protocols which can be realised in a fixed spacetime. Interestingly, only demanding no superluminal signalling (the weaker condition) does not rule out the possibility of genuine causal loops being embedded in Minkowski spacetime as shown in
VilasiniColbeckPRL
. In the language of our formalism, such genuine loops would correspond to those where the maximally fine-grained description is also cyclic i.e., the causal loop of
VilasiniColbeckPRL
is a counter-example which shows that
Theorem

5
would not hold under the weaker compatibility condition.
VilasiniColbeckPRL
does not suggest that such loops are physical in any way, rather, the main message of
VilasiniColbeckPRL
is that the principle of no superluminal signalling is not sufficient for ruling out causal loops in Minkowski spacetime, as commonly believed. In light of this, it remains important to understand the physical principles that can rule them out. Our work suggests that the stronger relativistic causality constraint (which is necessary for ruling out superluminal causation) is sufficient for ruling out such genuine causal loops in a fixed spacetime, at least in quantum theory. Whether this is also a necessary condition for ruling out such loops (even in quantum and classical theories) in a fixed spacetime, and whether the sufficiency result generalises to post-quantum theories remain open questions. In
Fig.

17
we illustrate the information-theoretic causal model of the causal loop proposed in
VilasiniColbeckPRL
, the cyclic network by which we can model it within the present framework and the spacetime embedding proposed in
VilasiniColbeckPRL
whereby the causal loop does not lead to superluminal signalling. While we have discussed some of the relationships and distinctions between the two formalisms, characterising the precise relationship(s) between the classes of causal loops proposed in
VilasiniColbeckPRA
;
VilasiniColbeckPRL
and the cyclic quantum networks proposed here, specifically the subset that describes indefinite causal structure processes, remains an interesting open question.

We stress that although our compatibility condition is stronger than the condition of
VilasiniColbeckPRA
;
VilasiniColbeckPRL
and is necessary for ensuring the absence of superluminal causation, it is formulated only at the level of the operational signalling structure of the network and without referring to the underlying causal decompositions of the maps involved. This is because the signalling, although distinct from causation, does allow us to operationally infer the presence of certain causal influences.
For the questions of interest in the present work, namely for understanding the physical and experimental realisations of indefinite causal structures in spacetime, the stronger compatibility condition that we have used is the more relevant one, as we are not yet aware of the existence of any physical mechanisms for superluminal causation in Minkowski spacetime. However, one way to evade the consequences of our main no-go theorems would be through a theoretical model which incorporates such superluminal causal mechanisms (which, could in-principle be possible without violating the principle of superluminal signalling). However, such causal explanations are typically disfavoured as they would need to be fine-tuned
Wood2015
;
VilasiniColbeckJamming
.

Appendix J
Proofs of all results from the main text

See
1

Proof.

In our framework, a signalling structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
of some network
\mathfrak{N}
(which will simply be denoted as
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
in the rest of the proof) is in general a directed graph where the nodes are subsets of in/output systems in the network, i.e.,
\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}})\subseteq\Sigma(\mathfrak{N}^{sys})
. An embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
in a spacetime
\mathcal{T}
corresponds to an assignment of spacetime regions to each system in
\mathfrak{N}^{sys}
. This immediately implies an embedding for all systems in
\Sigma(\mathfrak{N}^{sys})
: for any subset
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathfrak{N}^{sys}
the spacetime region assigned to
\mathcal{S}
through the embedding
\mathcal{E}
is simply the union
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{R}^{S}
of all the spacetime regions assigned to the individual elements
S\in\mathcal{S}
under the embedding
\mathcal{E}
. Thus, in order to establish the theorem statement, we first find an embedding for systems in
\mathfrak{N}^{sys}
, such that the signalling relations in
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
over the nodes corresponding to systems in
\mathfrak{N}^{sys}
respects relativistic causality. We will later see that this immediately implies an embedding of all the nodes of
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
such that relativistic causality is still preserved. Therefore, for the purpose of the next few paragraphs, we will treat
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
as a directed graph
\mathcal{G}
over the nodes
\mathfrak{N}^{sys}
with directed edges
\longrightarrow
, and generalise the result to the powerset
\Sigma(\mathfrak{N}^{sys})
at the end.

We first need to set out some nomenclature. For every node
N
of a directed graph
\mathcal{G}
, let the set
\mathrm{Par}(N):=\{N^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}):N^{\prime}\longrightarrow N\in\mathcal{G}\}
denote the set of all parents of the node
N
, the set
\mathrm{Ch}(N):=\{N^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}):N\longrightarrow N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\}
denote the set of all children of the node
N
, the set
\mathrm{Anc}(N):=\{N^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}):\exists\text{ directed path }N^{\prime}\longrightarrow...\longrightarrow N\in\mathcal{G}\}
denote the set of all ancestors of
N
in
\mathcal{G}
and the set
\mathrm{Desc}(N):=\{N^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}):\exists\text{ directed path }N\longrightarrow...\longrightarrow N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\}
denote the set of all descendants of
N
in
\mathcal{G}
. Then the set of all nodes in
\mathcal{G}
that are involved in at least one cycle is defined as

\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})=\{N\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}):N\in\mathrm{Anc}(N)\}.

That is, every node
N
that is its own ancestor belongs to the set
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
. Note that since
N_{1}\in\mathrm{Anc}(N_{2})
is equivalent to
N_{2}\in\mathrm{Desc}(N_{1})
, we could have equivalently defined this set as
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})=\{N\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}):N\in\mathrm{Desc}(N)\}
. In particular this implies that
N\in\mathrm{Anc}(N)\cap\mathrm{Desc}(N)
(or
N
is contained in its own strongly connected component
Bongers2021
), for every
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}
is a directed acyclic graph if and only if
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})=\emptyset
.

The rest of the proof proceeds as follow. We will show that we can fine-grain (cf.
Definition

2
) any signalling structure
\mathcal{G}
into a directed acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by “splitting” nodes in
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
, such that when the split nodes in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are recombined, we get back the original graph
\mathcal{G}
. Once we have a directed acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we can always embed it in a partially ordered spacetime
\mathcal{T}
though an embedding
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}
that respects relativistic causality. We can then coarse-grain the embedding
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
to an embedding
\mathcal{E}
of the original structure
\mathcal{G}
that also respects relativistic causality, which will establish the theorem statement.

Construct a new directed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from the original directed graph
\mathcal{G}
as follows. If
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})=\emptyset
, set
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}
. If
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})\neq\emptyset
, then split every node
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
into two nodes
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
such that
\mathrm{Par}(N_{1})=\mathrm{Par}(N)\backslash\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathrm{Ch}(N_{1})=\mathrm{Ch}(N)
and
\mathrm{Par}(N_{2})=\mathrm{Par}(N)
and
\mathrm{Ch}(N_{2})=\mathrm{Ch}(N)\backslash\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
i.e.,
N_{1}
contains no incoming arrows from nodes in
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
but all the same outgoing arrows as
N
does in
\mathcal{G}
while
N_{2}
contains no outgoing arrows to nodes in
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
but all the same incoming arrows as
N
does in
\mathcal{G}
. Nodes that do not belong to
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
, and other edges not featuring in the above construction remain unaffected. This fully defines
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
constructed in this way is a fine-graining of
\mathcal{G}
(cf.
Definition

2
). More precisely, when we recombine
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
back into a single node
N
, for each pair of split nodes and without altering the edge structure, we recover the original graph
\mathcal{G}
since
\mathrm{Par}(N_{1}\cup N_{2})=\mathrm{Par}(N)
and
\mathrm{Ch}(N_{1}\cup N_{2})=\mathrm{Ch}(N)
for every
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
, and the nodes
N\not\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
were not split or altered in going from
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. This implies that we can define a graph fine-graining
\mathcal{F}^{\text{graph}}:\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G})\mapsto\Sigma(\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}))
such that
\mathcal{F}^{\text{graph}}(N)=\{N_{1},N_{2}\}
for all
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathcal{F}^{\text{graph}}(N)=N
otherwise, with respect to which
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a fine-graining of
\mathcal{G}
.

Now, we can show that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a directed acyclic graph. For this, observe that a node of a graph can belong to the cycle of the graph only if it has a parent and a child that belong to the cycle of the graph.
This is because
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
implies that there is a directed path from
N
to itself in
\mathcal{G}
which implies a directed path from
N
to at least one
N^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Par}(N)
and this directed path together with
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow N
tells us that
N^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
. Similarly,
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
implies a directed path from at least one
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}(N)
to
N
and this directed path together with
N\longrightarrow N^{\prime\prime}
implies that
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
. Next, notice that if
N\not\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
, then by construction, we have
N\not\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
. Moreover, for each
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
, we have a pair of nodes
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
where all parents of
N_{1}
and all children of
N_{2}
are nodes of the form
N\not\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
(and hence
N\not\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
). Then, it follows that no node
N
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
can have both a parent and a child in
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
, and therefore that
N
itself cannot belong to
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
. In other words, this establishes that
\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})=\emptyset
or that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is acyclic.

Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a directed acyclic graph, there exists an embedding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\mapsto\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
in a partially ordered set
\mathcal{T}
(associated with the order relation
\prec
) such that
N_{i}\longrightarrow N_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(N_{i})\prec\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(N_{j})
. By virtue of being a partial order,
\mathcal{T}
satisfies our minimal definition of spacetime structure, according to Definition
18
.
Then the required embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{G}
in the spacetime
\mathcal{T}
simply associates two spacetime locations with each node
N\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
, the two locations being precisely those assigned by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
to each of the split nodes i.e.,
\mathcal{E}(N):=\{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(N_{1}),\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(N_{2})\}
. For all nodes
N\not\in\mathrm{Cycle}(\mathcal{G})
,
\mathcal{E}(N)=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(N)
noting that these nodes never got split. Then it is clear that the embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{G}
respects relativistic causality whenever the embedding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
respects relativistic causality (which it does by construction).

We now describe how the proof generalises to case where
\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G})=\Sigma(\mathfrak{N}^{sys})
. For this, note that our above proof covers all cases where
\mathcal{G}
has the property that
\mathcal{S}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{2}
for two subsets
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2}
of
\mathfrak{N}^{sys}
, then there exists
S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
S_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
such that
S_{1}\longrightarrow S_{2}
. However, suppose that we have a signalling relation
\mathcal{S}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{2}
in
\mathcal{G}
such that there is no signalling relation between individual elements of these two sets (this is indeed possible, see for instance
Fig.

5
). The relativistic causality condition implied by this signalling relation on the corresponding spacetime embedding is that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}_{2}}
(cf. Definition
23
). Since
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}_{1}}=\bigcup_{S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathcal{R}^{S_{1}}
(and similarly for
\mathcal{S}_{2}
), this is equivalent to saying that there exists
S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
S_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
such that the corresponding spacetime regions satisfy
\mathcal{R}^{S_{1}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{R}^{S_{2}}
. In other words, the relativistic causality constraints on the spacetime embedding of
\mathcal{G}
are the same irrespective of whether or not
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the aforementioned property. Thus the above proof also applies to establish the theorem statement for signalling structures
\mathcal{G}
not satisfying this property and hence applies to all signalling structures.

Finally, we note that according to Definition
18
any partially ordered set corresponds to a spacetime. This rather minimal definition allows us to derive general results that only depend on the order relation between spacetime points and does not require the spacetime to have any further symmetries, or a smooth differentiable structure. However, under this minimal definition, one might regard two different partially ordered sets
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
as two different “spacetimes”. On the other hand, if we consider the more standard method of modelling spacetime as a differentiable manifold
\mathcal{M}
, as done in relativistic physics, we could sample different sets of points on the same manifold to generate different partially ordered sets
17
17

17

That is, if the manifold is globally hyperbolic, in more exotic spacetimes with closed timelike curves, we can also obtain pre-ordered sets from sampling suitable points.

\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
from the same spacetime. If we model spacetime as a globally hyperbolic manifold that ensures the absence of closed timelike curves, then the statement of the present theorem would instead become “For every signalling structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
and every globally hyperbolic manifold
\mathcal{M}
, there exists an embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\text{sig}}
in a region causal structure

\mathcal{G}^{R}_{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mathcal{M}
that respects relativistic causality, where each node of
\mathcal{G}^{R}_{\mathcal{M}}
is a finite set of points in
\mathcal{M}
.” This can be shown as follows. If
\mathcal{G}
is a directed acyclic graph, then it can be embedded in any globally hyperbolic manifold
\mathcal{M}
through an embedding
\mathcal{E}:\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G})\mapsto\mathcal{M}
that assigns a point in
\mathcal{M}
to each node of
\mathcal{G}
(see
Paunkovic2019
for an explicit construction of such an embedding for the acyclic case). This is because the graph has a finite number of nodes and we can always sample a suitable set of points in the manifold having the required order relations. One can apply this embedding to the acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
constructed in the proof above, this would define the embedding
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}:\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G})\mapsto\mathcal{M}
. The rest of the proof will be the same as the above case for partially ordered sets
\mathcal{T}
.

Recall from

that the induced map of the network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
which we denote by

is given as follows

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}:=\Big(\mathcal{W}\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}\Big)^{\{A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k},A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}},

where

is an

and outputs

and classical input

(for the setting

) and quantum output

and a classical output

(for the outcome

). Here the primed and corresponding unprimed systems are isomorphic to each other, which allows them to be loop composed together. Then the map

obtained this way has

and

. The above equation can be read as a parallel composition of the process map with the

local maps followed by

loop compositions.

In the following, for brevity, we detail the proof for the bipartite case. However, the proof readily generalises to the
N
agent case.
In the bipartite case, taking the agents to be
A
and
B
with local settings associated with input systems
A^{s}
,
B^{s}
and outcomes associated with output systems
A^{o}
,
B^{o}
, the parallel composition yields the map
\mathcal{M}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{W}
with input systems
\{A^{{}^{\prime}I},A^{s},B^{{}^{\prime}I},B^{s},A^{{}^{\prime}O},B^{{}^{\prime}O}\}
and output systems
\{A^{O},A^{o},B^{O},B^{o},A^{I},B^{I}\}
.
Applying the loop formula of
Eq.

1
to compose the corresponding prime and unprimed systems, we obtain the following expression for the induced map
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}
acting on an input state
|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|^{B^{s}}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|^{B^{s}})\\
&=\sum_{k...r}\langle kmoq|^{A^{I}A^{O}B^{I}B^{O}}\Bigg(\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\big(|k\rangle\langle l|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\big)\otimes\mathcal{M}^{B}_{b}\big(|o\rangle\langle p|^{B^{{}^{\prime}I}}\big)\otimes\mathcal{W}\big(|mq\rangle\langle nr|^{A^{{}^{\prime}O}B^{{}^{\prime}O}}\big)\Bigg)|lnpr\rangle^{A^{I}A^{O}B^{I}B^{O}}\\

where we have used the notation
\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\big(|k\rangle\langle l|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\big):=\mathcal{M}^{A}\big(|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|k\rangle\langle l|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\big)
(and similarly for

by
(...)
, introducing factors of the identity
\mathcal{I}=\sum_{j}|j\rangle\langle j|
, and then rearranging the resulting inner products we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|^{B^{s}})\\
&=\sum_{ijst}\sum_{k...r}\langle k|^{A^{I}}\langle n|j\rangle\langle j|^{A^{O}}\langle o|^{B^{I}}\langle r|s\rangle\langle s|^{B^{O}}\Bigg(...\Bigg)|i\rangle^{A^{I}}\langle i|l\rangle^{A^{I}}|m\rangle^{A^{O}}|t\rangle^{B^{I}}\langle t|p\rangle^{B^{I}}|q\rangle^{B^{O}}\\
&=\sum_{ijst}\sum_{k...r}\langle i|^{A^{I}}|l\rangle\langle k|^{A^{I}}\langle j|^{A^{O}}\langle t|^{B^{I}}|p\rangle\langle o|^{B^{I}}\langle s|^{B^{O}}\Bigg(...\Bigg)|i\rangle^{A^{I}}|m\rangle\langle n|^{A^{O}}|j\rangle^{A^{O}}|t\rangle^{B^{I}}|q\rangle\langle r|^{B^{O}}|s\rangle^{B^{O}}\\
&=\operatorname{Tr}_{A^{I}A^{O}B^{I}B^{O}}\Bigg[\sum_{k...r}|l\rangle\langle k|^{A^{I}}\otimes|p\rangle\langle o|^{B^{I}}\otimes\Bigg(...\Bigg)\otimes|m\rangle\langle n|^{A^{O}}\otimes|q\rangle\langle r|^{B^{O}}\Bigg]\\
&=\operatorname{Tr}_{A^{I}A^{O}B^{I}B^{O}}\Bigg[\Bigg(\sum_{kl}|l\rangle\langle k|^{A^{I}}\otimes\big[\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\big(|k\rangle\langle l|^{A^{I}}\big)\big]^{T}\Bigg)\otimes\Bigg(\sum_{op}|p\rangle\langle o|^{B^{I}}\otimes\big[\mathcal{M}^{B}_{b}\big(|o\rangle\langle p|^{B^{I}}\big)\big]^{T}\Bigg)\\
&\qquad\qquad\otimes\Bigg(\sum_{mnqr}|mq\rangle\langle nr|^{A^{O}B^{O}}\otimes\mathcal{W}\big(|mq\rangle\langle nr|^{A^{O}B^{O}}\big)\Bigg)\Bigg]\end{split}

Now, we wish to calculate the probability that the output of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|^{B^{s}})
is
|x\rangle^{A^{o}}\otimes|y\rangle^{B^{o}}
i.e., the outcomes
x
and
y
are obtained by Alice and Bob upon measuring the settings
a
and
b
. This is given by
Eq.

13
. We now show that the numerator of this expression i.e,
\operatorname{Tr}\Bigg[\Big(|x\rangle\langle x|^{A^{o}}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|^{B^{o}}\Big)\Big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|^{B^{s}})\Big)\Bigg]
equals the process matrix probability rule of
Eq.

8
.
Using the expression for
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(|a\rangle\langle a|^{A^{s}}\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|^{B^{s}})
derived above, along with
Eq.

11
to absorb the outcome projectors into the definition of the maps
\mathcal{M}_{x|a}^{A}
and
\mathcal{M}_{y|b}^{B}
, we immediately obtain the following which coincides with
Eq.

8
.

P(xy|ab)=\operatorname{Tr}\left[\left(M_{x|a}^{A^{I}A^{O}}\otimes M_{y|b}^{B^{I}B^{O}}\right)W\right],

(48)

where
W=\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{W}|\mathds{1}\rrangle\llangle\mathds{1}|
is the process matrix and
M_{x|a}^{A^{I}A^{O}}=\Big[\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{A}_{x|a}\Big(|\mathds{1}\rrangle\llangle\mathds{1}|\Big)\Big]^{T}
is the Choi matrix of the local map
\mathcal{M}^{A}_{x|a}
(and similarly for
B
’s operation) as defined in Section
VI
. Whenever
W
is valid process matrix, this distribution is normalised, which implies that
\sum_{x}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\left(M_{x|a}^{A^{I}A^{O}}\otimes M_{y|b}^{B^{I}B^{O}}\right)W\right]=1
, which in turn implies that the denominator of
Eq.

13
equals unity.

∎

See
3

Proof.

The proof method is very similar to that of Lemma
2
but we provide it here for completeness and follow the same notation as the previous proof. Again, we restrict to the bipartite case for simplicity but the proof easily generalises to the multi-partite case. Consider a bipartite process map
\mathcal{W}
associated with the agents
A_{1}\equiv A
and
A_{2}\equiv B
, with input systems
A^{{}^{\prime}O}
,
B^{O}
and output systems
A^{I}
,
B^{I}
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}:A^{{}^{\prime}I}\mapsto A^{O}
be a local operation of the agent
A
associated with a particular setting
a
, and where all the primed systems are isomorphic to the corresponding unprimed ones (we only add the primes on
A
’s systems as we will consider only their composition in the next steps).
The sub-network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},1<2}
(which we denote here as
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}
) formed by composing
\mathcal{W}
only with the operation of
A
, through
A^{I}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I},A^{O}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}
will have an induced map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}
which is associated with the input
B^{O}
and output
B^{I}
. We now show that for every choice of setting
a
, the Choi matrix of the induced sub-network map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}
is the reduced process matrix
\overline{W}(M_{a}^{A^{I}A^{O}})
over
B
(cf. Equation (
14
)).
This will complete the proof as the same arguments, under exchange of
A
and
B
apply for showing that the Choi matrix of the submetwork
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},B}
is the reduced process matrix
\overline{W}(M_{b}^{B^{I}B^{O}})
on
A
, and these two are the only non-trivial reduced processes here. The Choi matrix of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}
is given as

\sum_{m,n}|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}(|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}}).

Using the composition operation, we can write
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}(|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}})
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}(|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}})&=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\langle i|^{A^{I}}\langle k|^{A^{O}}\Big(\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\otimes\mathcal{W}\Big)\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\otimes|km\rangle\langle ln|^{A^{{}^{\prime}O}B^{O}}\Big)|j\rangle^{A^{I}}|l\rangle^{A^{O}}\\
&=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\langle k|^{A^{O}}\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\Big)|l\rangle^{A^{O}}\otimes\langle i|^{A^{I}}\mathcal{W}\Big(|km\rangle\langle ln|^{A^{{}^{\prime}O}B^{O}}\Big)|j\rangle^{A^{I}}\end{split}

Plugging this back into the Choi matrix, inserting factors of the identity and rearranging, we can write the Choi matrix of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i,j,k,l,m,n}|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}}\otimes\langle k|^{A^{O}}\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\Big)|l\rangle^{A^{O}}\otimes\langle i|^{A^{I}}\mathcal{W}\Big(|km\rangle\langle ln|^{A^{{}^{\prime}O}B^{O}}\Big)|j\rangle^{A^{I}}\\
=&\sum_{i,j,k,l,m,n}|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}}\otimes\langle l|^{A^{O}}\Big[\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|^{A^{{}^{\prime}I}}\Big)\Big]^{T}|k\rangle^{A^{O}}\otimes\langle i|^{A^{I}}\mathcal{W}\Big(|km\rangle\langle ln|^{A^{{}^{\prime}O}B^{O}}\Big)|j\rangle^{A^{I}}\\
=&\sum_{i,j,k,l,m,n,p,q}|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}}\otimes\langle l|^{A^{O}}|p\rangle\langle p|^{A^{O}}\Big[\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|\Big)\Big]^{T}|k\rangle^{A^{O}}\otimes\langle i|^{A^{I}}|q\rangle\langle q|^{A^{I}}\mathcal{W}\Big(|km\rangle\langle ln|\Big)|j\rangle^{A^{I}}\\
=&\sum_{i,j,k,l,m,n,p,q}|m\rangle\langle n|^{B^{O}}\otimes\langle p|^{A^{O}}\Big[\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|\Big)\Big]^{T}|k\rangle^{A^{O}}\langle l|p\rangle^{A^{O}}\otimes\langle q|^{A^{I}}\mathcal{W}\Big(|km\rangle\langle ln|\Big)|j\rangle^{A^{I}}\langle i|q\rangle^{A^{I}}\\
=&\sum_{p,q}\langle pq|^{A^{O}A^{I}}\Bigg(\mathds{1}^{B^{I}B^{O}}\otimes\sum_{i,j}|j\rangle\langle i|\otimes\Big[\mathcal{M}^{A}_{a}\Big(|i\rangle\langle j|\Big)\Big]^{T}\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{k,l,m,n}|km\rangle\langle ln|\otimes\mathcal{W}\Big(|km\rangle\langle ln|\Big)\Bigg)|pq\rangle^{A^{O}A^{I}}\\
=&\Tr_{A^{I}A^{O}}\Bigg(\Big(\mathds{1}^{B^{I}B^{O}}\otimes M^{A^{I}A^{O}}_{a}\Big).W\Bigg)=\overline{W}(M^{A^{I}A^{O}}_{a}),\end{split}

where we have used the definition of the Choi matrices of the local operations and the process map (see Section
VI
) in the last line, along with that of the reduced process matrix (Equation (
14
)). Moreover, we have identified the primed and unprimed versions of the systems
A^{I}
and
A^{O}
as they get composed to form effectively a single system. Moreover the reduced process matrix
\overline{W}(M^{A^{I}A^{O}}_{a})
being a valid process matrix implies that the map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},A}
of which it is the Choi matrix is a CPTP map
Araujo2015
. This completes the proof.
∎

See
2

Proof.

The sub-networks of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
are formed by taking subsets of its maps and compositions (cf.
Eq.

12
). Subsets of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}^{maps}
are of two types: either the subset includes
\mathcal{W}
or it does not. In the latter case, none of the compositions of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}^{comp}
can be applied, as all compositions involve
\mathcal{W}
. Thus such sub-networks are always guaranteed to be CPTP since they correspond to the parallel composition (i.e., tensor product) of CPTP maps, and we only need to consider sub-networks formed by composing
\mathcal{W}
with some subset of the extended local operations through some subset of the compositions in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}^{comp}
. W.l.o.g, consider the subset of the first
l<N
extended local operations. The compositions associated with these operations and
\mathcal{W}
are
\{A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k},A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{l}
. If we were to perform all these compositions, we obtain the sub-network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},l<N}
, whose induced map is CPTP as it corresponds to a reduced process matrix associated with the remaining
N-l
agents, as we have shown in
Lemma

3
.

Suppose we perform only a subset of the compositions in
\{A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k},A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{l}
. Consider all the agents
A_{k}
for which both compositions
\{A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k},A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}\}
are included in this subset. Again, w.l.o.g, we can take these to be the first
p<l
agents. As the order in which we perform the compositions does not matter (cf.
Lemma

6
), we can perform all the operations in the subset under consideration by first performing the compositions
\{A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k},A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}
and then the rest. By
Lemma

3
if again follows that performing the compositions
\{A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k},A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}
results in a CPTP map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},p<N}
. Now by construction, the remaining compositions, include at most one of
A^{I}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}I}_{k}
or
A^{O}_{k}\hookrightarrow A^{{}^{\prime}O}_{k}
for each agent in
\{A_{p+1},...,A_{l}\}
. It is easy to see that each of these compositions would correspond to a regular sequential composition of the CPTP map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{W},p<N}
with a CPTP map
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}
for an agent
A_{k}
in
\{A_{p+1},...,A_{l}\}
. The sequential composition of two CPTP maps (in any given order) is always a CPTP map. This shows that the remaining compositions also yield a CPTP map. We have covered all possible sub-networks in the above and shown their induced maps to be CPTP. This establishes that our original network is a network of CPTP maps, as required.
∎

See
3

Proof.

Observe that in the network
\mathfrak{N}_{W,N}
, we have
\{A_{k}^{I}\}
signals to
\{A_{k}^{O}\}
for every agent
A_{k}
since the extended local map for each agent encodes all possible operations between their these in and output systems and in particular the identity channel which clearly signals from the input to output. This implies that when we compatibly embed (
Definition

14
) the systems of
\mathfrak{N}_{W,N}
in a causal structure
\mathcal{G}
with directed edges
\xrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
(thereby allowing us to treat each system as a node of a directed graph with these edges), then we must have
A^{I}_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}A^{O}_{k}
for each
k\in\{1,...,N\}
. By
Definition

28
of fixed ordered processes and
Definition

29
, we can see that a process
W
is a fixed order process if any only if there exists a partial order on the in/output systems of all agents where
A^{O}_{i}
precedes
A^{I}_{j}
in the partial order for some
j\in\mathcal{S}
whenever an agent
A_{i}
probabilistically signals to a set of agents
A_{\mathcal{S}}
, and
A^{I}_{k}
precedes
A^{O}_{k}
in this partial order for every agent
A_{k}
. By
Theorem

7
,
A_{i}
probabilistically signals to
A_{\mathcal{S}}
is equivalent to
\{A^{O}_{i}\}
signals to
\{A^{I}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{S}}
in the network. Recall that compatibility of
a signalling relation
\{A^{O}_{i}\}
signals to
\{A^{I}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{S}}
with an embedding in a causal structure
\mathcal{G}
implies that there exists
j\in\mathcal{S}
such that there is a directed path
A^{O}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}A^{I}_{j}
with respect to the order induced by
\mathcal{G}
on the embedded systems (cf.
Definition

14
). Then the above arguments, taken together imply that
W
is a fixed order process if and only if there exists a directed acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}
with which the signalling relations of the process network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
are compatible. Equivalently
W
is not a fixed order process if and only if any directed graph
\mathcal{G}
with which the signalling relations of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
are compatible is a directed cyclic graph i.e., the signalling relations of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
being compatible with an embedding in some
\mathcal{G}
certify the cyclicity of
\mathcal{G}
whenever
W
is not a fixed order process.

∎

See
4

Proof.

Recall that a spacetime realisation of a network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
is specified by a spacetime embedding
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
of the network (obtained by associating a spacetime region
\mathcal{E}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{T}
for each system
S
in the network) along with a fine-graining
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
. For this theorem, the fine-graining and its properties will be irrelevant, we only need to focus on the spacetime embedded network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
to prove the result. Observe that the relativistic causality condition of
Definition

24
implies, in particular, that the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
must be compatible with the region causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
induced by the embedding
\mathcal{E}
. Moreover, the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
is, by construction,
the same as the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
, since the only difference in the two networks is in the relabelling of each system
S
to the corresponding spacetime embedded system
S^{\mathcal{E}(S)}
. This implies that the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
must be compatible with the causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
if condition 2 of the theorem must hold. Now if we impose condition 1, that the associated process
W
is not a fixed order process, it immediately follows from
Theorem

3
that
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
must contain a directed cycle and therefore that it is not an acyclic graph, which would violate condition 3. This proves the theorem.
∎

See
2

Proof.

This corollary follows from noting that the first two assumptions are identical to the first two assumptions of Theorem
4
, while the third assumption here implies the third assumption of the theorem. More explicitly, assumption 3 here requires that for each system
S
, the corresponding spacetime region
\mathcal{R}^{S}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
assigned to
S
by the embedding
\mathcal{E}
is such that all spacetime points in
\mathcal{R}^{S}
have the same time coordinate, say
t^{S}
in a global reference frame. Then, Definitions
18
and
19
tell us that
\mathcal{R}^{S_{1}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{R}^{S_{2}}

implies
t^{S_{1}}<t^{S_{2}}
. Since we can never have a sequence of times in some global reference frame such that
t^{S_{1}}<t^{S_{2}}<...<t^{S_{n}}<t^{S_{1}}
, it follows that the set of spacetime regions satisfying assumption 3 of this corollary can never contain a sequence of regions such that
\mathcal{R}^{S_{1}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{R}^{S_{2}}\xrightarrow{R}...\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{R}^{S_{n}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{R}^{S_{1}}
, i.e., the regions satisfy assumption 3 of Theorem
4
.
∎

See
5

Proof.

We prove the bipartite case here for simplicity, but the proof method readily generalises to the
N
-partite case. Let
W
be a bipartite process matrix associated with the agents
A
and
B
and
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
be the associated network with
N=2
. Consider a spacetime embedding
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
in a fixed acyclic spacetime
\mathcal{T}
. If the region causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
induced by the embedding
\mathcal{E}
is acyclic, then this spacetime embedding fully specifies a spacetime realisation of the network (
Definition

22
). In this case, if we impose the first condition of the theorem, namely that relativistic causality condition of
Definition

24
is satisfied, then it follows that the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
is compatible with the causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
(which is acyclic in the case under consideration). This signalling structure is the same as the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
(since the spacetime embedded network only differs from the original network in the fact that each system
S
is additionally labelled by the corresponding spacetime region
\mathcal{E}(S)
), which implies that the signalling structure of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
is compatible with an acyclic causal structure, which is only possible if
W
is a fixed order process (cf.
Theorem

3
).

Therefore we consider the case where
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is cyclic. In this case, we require a fine-graining
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
, associated with an acyclic region causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}}
in order to fully specify the spacetime realisation. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}}
would be a fine-graining of the region causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
induced by the original embedding
\mathcal{E}
. Invoking the input-output correspondence between the regions in the embedding, we can denote by
\mathcal{R}^{A^{I}}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}})
the region assigned to
A
’s input systems
A^{s}
and
A^{I}
by
\mathcal{E}
and by
\mathcal{R}^{A^{O}}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}})
the region assigned to
A
’s output systems
A^{o}
and
A^{O}
, and similarly for
B
. Further, since
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}}
is a fine-graining of
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}}
, there is a fine-graining map that maps
\mathcal{R}^{A^{I}}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}})
to a set of nodes
\{\mathcal{P}^{I}_{1},...,\mathcal{P}^{I}_{n}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}})
which partition it,
\mathcal{R}^{A^{I}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{I}_{j}
. The input-output correspondence ensures that
\mathcal{R}^{A^{O}}\in\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg}}_{\mathcal{T}})
is mapped to a corresponding set of nodes
\{\mathcal{P}^{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{P}^{O}_{n}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}})
under the fine-graining, which partition it as
\mathcal{R}^{A^{O}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{O}_{j}
and where
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{i}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{P}^{O}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,...,n\}
. Similarly, we have the set of regions
\{\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{1},..,\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{m}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}})
associated with Bob’s input
B^{I}
under the fine-graining, and a corresponding set of regions
\{\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{1},..,\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{m}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Nodes}(\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}})
associated with
B^{O}
, where
\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{j}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{j}
for each
j\in\{1,...,m\}
, with
\mathcal{R}^{B^{I}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{j}
and
\mathcal{R}^{B^{O}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{j}
.

Then in the fine-grained network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
, we have
n
input and
n
output systems associated with Alice (an input system for each
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{i}
and an output system for each
\mathcal{P}^{O}_{i}
) and
m
input and
m
output systems associated with Bob (an input system for each
\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{j}
and an output system for each
\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{j}
). Moreover, since
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
is a fine-graining of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
, each sub-network of the former must be a fine-graining of a corresponding sub-network of the latter (
Definition

17
). As the process map
\mathcal{W}
itself corresponds to a sub-network of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
, the fine-grained network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
must be associated with a fine-grained process map
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
whose inputs are the
n
output systems of Alice, along with the
m
output systems of Bob, and whose outputs are the
n
input systems of Alice and the
m
input systems of Bob. We then split Alice into
n
and Bob into
m
agents, associated with the corresponding in/output systems in order to model
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
as an
n+m
-partite process map. In the following we show that whenever the spacetime realisation satisfies the relativistic causality condition,
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
is indeed a valid
n+m
-partite process map which corresponds to a fixed order process.

In
Corollary

6
we have shown that spacetime realisations of any network that respect the relativistic causality condition correspond to so-called
causal boxes
which are CPTPMs that are closed under arbitrary compositions.
Imposing relativistic causality for the spacetime realisation of the original network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
, this result implies that the induced map of each sub-network of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
must be a causal box and in particular that
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
is a causal box. This implies that composing
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
together with any choice of extended local operations (which are themselves CPTP maps) between each input-output pair from the
n+m
inputs associated with
\{\mathcal{P}^{I}_{1},...,\mathcal{P}_{n}^{I},\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{1},...,\mathcal{Q}^{I}_{m}\}
and
n+m
outputs associated with
\{\mathcal{P}^{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{P}_{n}^{O},\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{Q}^{O}_{m}\}
can only lead to valid normalised probabilities. This composition is also a valid composition of causal boxes as the input regions are ordered before the corresponding output regions relative to the partial order induced by the acyclic region causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}}
. From this argument, we conclude that
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
is a valid
n+m
-partite process map whenever the given spacetime realisation of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
satisfies the relativistic causality condition. Then it immediately follows from
Theorem

3
, just as in the first paragraph of this proof, that
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
must be a fixed-order process as its signalling structure is compatible with an acyclic causal structure
\mathcal{G}^{\text{reg},f}_{\mathcal{T}}
(which is implied by the relativistic causality condition).

Finally, we note that
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}^{f}
being a fine-graining of
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W},N}
also implies that the extended local maps
\mathcal{M}^{A}
and
\mathcal{M}^{B}
of Alice and Bob in the original network get fine-grained to extended local maps
\mathcal{M}^{A,f}
and
\mathcal{M}^{B,f}
acting on
n
and
m
in/output systems respectively.
As we have not placed any constraints on the fine-grained local maps, these need not factorise as
\mathcal{M}^{A,f}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}^{A,f}_{i}
with each
\mathcal{M}^{A,f}_{i}
acting between the in/output systems associated with
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{O}\xleftarrow{R}\mathcal{P}^{I}_{i}
. Thus the
n+m
agents in the fine-grained process can perform non-product local operations by communicating to each other. The fact that the spacetime realisation satisfying relativistic causality can be described as a causal box guarantees that the process
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E}}
will lead to valid normalised probabilities even when composed with such non-product local operations. This completes the proof.

As an additional remark, we note that above argument using causal boxes also guarantees that
each fine-grained local operation will admit a decomposition into a sequential composition of CPTP maps (the sequence representation), whenever relativistic causality is imposed. This means that the local operations correspond to quantum combs (these are defined, for instance in
Chiribella2009
) and allows us to recover the product structure of the local operations through a simple trick originally proposed in
Hoffreumon2021
. One can always map a process and set of non-product local operations modelled as quantum combs (on which the action of the process leads to normalised probabilities) to a new process and a set of product local operations such that the composition is identical in the two cases, yielding the same correlations. This can be done by absorbing all communication channels in the non-product local operations into the definition of the new process.
∎

See
4

Proof.

We first show that in
\mathcal{W}_{QS}
, when the initial state of the control and target systems is
|\psi_{C}\rangle:=\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle
and
|\psi_{T}\rangle
respectively where
\alpha
and
\beta
are both non-zero amplitudes and
|\psi_{T}\rangle
is an arbitrary qudit state, then we have
\{A^{O}\}\longrightarrow\{B^{I}\}
irrespective of the state input on
B^{O}
and
\{B^{O}\}\longrightarrow\{A^{I}\}
irrespective of the state input on
A^{O}
, in
\mathcal{W}
. This implies that both signalling relations can be realised in the network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W}_{QS},U,V}
for any fixed choice of non-trivial operations
\mathcal{U}^{A}
and
\mathcal{V}^{B}
without needing to consider the extended local operations. This is in contrast to the general case of Theorem
4
where the signalling relation being realised may depend on the choice of local operations, such that not all signalling relations allowed by
\mathcal{W}
are realised when it is composed with a given fixed set of local operations.

Consider the action of the process map
\mathcal{W}_{QS}:C^{O}_{C}\otimes C^{O}_{T}\otimes A^{O}\otimes B^{O}\mapsto D^{I}_{C}\otimes D^{I}_{T}\otimes A^{I}\otimes B^{I}
on the input state
|\Psi\rangle^{C^{O}_{C}C^{O}_{T}A^{O}B^{O}}:=(\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle)^{C^{O}_{C}}\otimes|\psi_{T}\rangle^{C^{O}_{T}}\otimes|\psi_{A}\rangle^{A^{O}}\otimes|\psi_{B}\rangle^{B^{O}}
, where
|\psi_{A}\rangle
and
|\psi_{B}\rangle
are arbitrary qudit states. We see that
\mathcal{W}_{QS}
maps the initial state
|\Psi\rangle^{C^{O}_{C}C^{O}_{T}A^{O}B^{O}}
, to the final state

\alpha|0\rangle^{D^{I}_{C}}|\psi_{B}\rangle^{D^{I}_{T}}|\psi_{T}\rangle^{A^{I}}|\psi_{A}\rangle^{B^{I}}+\beta|1\rangle^{D^{I}_{C}}|\psi_{A}\rangle^{D^{I}_{T}}|\psi_{B}\rangle^{A^{I}}|\psi_{T}\rangle^{B^{I}}:=|\Phi\rangle^{D^{I}_{C}D^{I}_{T}A^{I}B^{I}}.

This gives,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Tr}_{D^{I}_{C}D^{I}_{T}A^{I}}\big[\mathcal{W}_{QS}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|^{C^{O}_{C}C^{O}_{T}A^{O}B^{O}})\big]&=|\alpha|^{2}|\psi_{A}\rangle\langle\psi_{A}|^{B^{I}}+|\beta|^{2}|\psi_{T}\rangle\langle\psi_{T}|^{B^{I}}\\
\operatorname{Tr}_{D^{I}_{C}D^{I}_{T}B^{I}}\big[\mathcal{W}_{QS}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|^{C^{O}_{C}C^{O}_{T}A^{O}B^{O}})\big]&=|\alpha|^{2}|\psi_{T}\rangle\langle\psi_{T}|^{A^{I}}+|\beta|^{2}|\psi_{B}\rangle\langle\psi_{B}|^{A^{I}}\end{split}

(49)

Notice that
\operatorname{Tr}_{D^{I}_{C}D^{I}_{T}A^{I}}\big[\mathcal{W}_{QS}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|)\big]
which is the output on
B^{I}
does depend on
\psi_{A}
, the input on
A^{O}
but not depend on
\psi_{B}
, the input on
B^{O}
. Similarly
\operatorname{Tr}_{D^{I}_{C}D^{I}_{T}B^{I}}\big[\mathcal{W}_{QS}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|)\big]
depends on
\psi_{B}
but does not depend on
\psi_{A}
. This implies that given the knowledge of the initial control and target states,
\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle
and
|\psi_{T}\rangle
, Alice and Bob can signal to each other by suitable choices of
|\psi_{A}\rangle
and
|\psi_{B}\rangle
on their respective output systems
A^{O}
and
B^{O}
, irrespective of the local operation of the other agent. The above proof easily generalises to arbitrary input states
\rho^{C^{O}_{C}C^{O}_{T}A^{O}B^{O}}:=|\psi_{C}\rangle\langle\psi_{C}|^{C^{O}_{C}}\otimes|\psi_{T}\rangle\langle\psi_{T}|^{C^{O}_{T}}\otimes\rho^{A^{O}B^{O}}
, where the input state
\rho^{A^{O}B^{O}}
on
A^{O}
and
B^{O}
may be an entangled state,
\operatorname{Tr}_{D^{I}_{C}D^{I}_{T}A^{I}}\big[\mathcal{W}_{QS}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|)\big]
depends only on the marginal of the initial state over
A^{O}
which is unaffected by local operations on
B^{O}
.

This establishes that the network
\mathfrak{N}_{\mathcal{W}_{QS},U,V}
gives rise to a directed cycle of signalling relations
\{A^{O}\}\longrightarrow\{B^{I}\}\longrightarrow\{B^{O}\}\longrightarrow\{A^{I}\}\longrightarrow\{A^{O}\}
for any non-trivial
\mathcal{U}^{A}
and
\mathcal{V}^{B}
(where
\{A^{I}\}\longrightarrow\{A^{O}\}
and
\{B^{I}\}\longrightarrow\{B^{O}\}
come from the non-triviality of these operations). Then implies (by relativistic causality) that the spacetime regions must satisfy
\mathcal{P}^{A^{O}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{P}^{B^{I}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{P}^{B^{O}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{P}^{A^{I}}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{P}^{A^{O}}
, which violates assumption 3. This completes the proof.

∎
